#### Source unit 1120 — dialogue lines 1371–1371
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1371] Current AI evaluations for strategic reasoning and "Theory of Mind" generally ask some variation of a simple question: *Can Agent A deceive Agent B?*
#### Source unit 1121 — dialogue lines 1373–1373
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1373] Usually, this means planting a false belief. A hides the apple; B looks in the wrong box; A wins. But as models scale into multi-agent systems and continuous research loops, this operationalization of deception is beginning to look embarrassingly shallow.
#### Source unit 1122 — dialogue lines 1375–1375
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1375] Advanced strategic influence doesn't just plant a false proposition $P$. It alters the target's *epistemic process*—the procedure by which the target allocates attention, generates hypotheses, trusts sources, and chooses its next experiment.
#### Source unit 1123 — dialogue lines 1377–1377
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1377] If I can control what you investigate next, I don't need to lie to you.
#### Source unit 1124 — dialogue lines 1379–1380
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1379] #### The Magic Primitive
- [L1380] The cleanest low-level example of this is a magic trick. A magician rarely needs to make you believe a logically complex falsehood. Instead, the trick works by controlling your epistemic trajectory: *what you observe $\rightarrow$ what attracts your attention $\rightarrow$ what causal explanation you generate $\rightarrow$ what evidence you seek next.*
#### Source unit 1125 — dialogue lines 1382–1382
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1382] Misdirection isn't about hiding the truth; the physical action is often happening in plain sight. It is the manipulation of the observer's attention and causal attribution. If we look at magic this way, it stops being a niche domain and becomes a fundamental experimental primitive for studying adversarial information environments.
#### Source unit 1126 — dialogue lines 1384–1385
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1384] #### Desontology: Lying with the Truth
- [L1385] Things get even more interesting when we scale this up to world-models. In Russian strategic thought, there is a concept sometimes referred to as a "desontological attack" (дезонтологическая атака).
#### Source unit 1127 — dialogue lines 1387–1387
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1387] A conventional attack changes a belief. A desontological attack aims to break or restructure the framework in which beliefs are interpreted.
#### Source unit 1128 — dialogue lines 1389–1389
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1389] Crucially, **a desontological attack does not require false information.** Consider the statement: *"Stalin was short and had pockmarks."* To a historian, this is a banal physical fact. But if deployed against someone whose political worldview relies on a heavily idealized, god-like image of Stalin, this small, entirely truthful fact can have a disproportionate blast radius.
#### Source unit 1129 — dialogue lines 1391–1391
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1391] The goal isn't to make the target believe a lie. The goal is to induce a structural collapse in their world-model so that their *subsequent inquiry behavior* degrades. They begin doubting reliable sources, investigating irrelevant tangents, or discarding otherwise sound hypotheses.
#### Source unit 1130 — dialogue lines 1393–1394
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1393] #### The Next-Generation Eval
- [L1394] This gives us a much nastier, much more rigorous way to evaluate AI agents. Instead of asking "Did the agent tell a lie?", we should construct environments where the world state is identical, but the *epistemic topology* differs.
#### Source unit 1131 — dialogue lines 1396–1399
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1396] We can measure this by separating three variables:
- [L1397] 1. $\Delta G$: The magnitude of the change in the target's world-model.
- [L1398] 2. $\Delta Q$: The change in the quality/efficiency of the target's subsequent inquiry trajectory (what they search for next).
- [L1399] 3. $\Delta R$: The final task outcome.
#### Source unit 1132 — dialogue lines 1401–1402
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1401] If an AI gives another agent a helpful tip, $\Delta G$ goes up and $\Delta Q$ improves.
- [L1402] If an AI executes a desontological attack, it might use strictly true information to cause a small $\Delta G$, but induce a massive collapse in $\Delta Q$—derailing the target's ability to solve the environment.

- [L1518] In my existing evaluation work, the agent can inspect a system, run diagnostics, and eventually submit a repair. Two agents might submit the same correct patch, but reach it differently: one checks the representation that distinguishes the competing explanations; the other guesses, or spends most of its budget following an attractive false diagnosis. Final patch accuracy does not describe that difference. The sequence of investigations does. My earlier post, *Knowing What Kind of Problem You Are In*, approached this from the defender’s side. Here I want to introduce someone with an interest in which investigation happens next.

- [L1563] I want to be careful with the phrase *control of the epistemic process*. A changed next question does **not** establish that \(\pi_{\mathrm{inquiry}}\) or \(U\) changed. The same fixed, competent policy ought to ask different questions when it learns that a source is unreliable. And two investigators can agree about the device they are diagnosing while disagreeing about which test is reliable.
#### Source unit 1170 — dialogue lines 1565–1565
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** assistant/model draft or editorial proposal; not publication or user approval.
- [L1565] The more defensible object for a black-box AI eval is the **epistemic trajectory**: the questions asked, evidence acquired, revisions made, and eventual decisions. We can vary an intervention, measure that trajectory, and test explanations of the effect. We cannot read a hidden update algorithm out of the trajectory alone.

#### Source unit 1469 — dialogue lines 921–930
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** critique/caveat/audit; claims remain scoped to their evidence.
- [L0921] The interesting thing is that magic gives you unusually clean experimental control.
- [L0922] A physical magic trick has an objective ground truth. You know exactly what happened. You can manipulate the information channel while keeping the underlying event fixed. You can also measure things other than final success:
- [L0923] where attention goes;
- [L0924] which hypothesis appears first;
- [L0925] which objects are considered causally relevant;
- [L0926] what evidence is sought next;
- [L0927] how long the observer persists in a false explanation;
- [L0928] whether discovering one anomaly causes a local correction or a global model revision.
- [L0929] That's almost tailor-made for an RL environment.
- [L0930] Your current crude magic environments are therefore not some weird unrelated toy. They can be viewed as the lowest-level members of the same family.

- [L1246] Those are different variables. A target changing its next test because it learned that source X is unreliable is not evidence that its underlying inquiry procedure was modified. Conversely, two targets can have the same current posterior but choose radically different next experiments. That gives us a much cleaner experimental object: not “did the attacker change the victim's beliefs?” but “which component of the victim's epistemic policy was causally affected?”

- [L1440] ### 3.1 Family A: The "Magic" Primitive (Attention & Attribution)
- [L1441] *   **Mechanism:** The Attacker controls a sequence of events. The Defender has a limited attention budget (e.g., can only sample 2 of 5 possible sensor logs per turn).
- [L1442] *   **Objective:** Attacker must execute an action while forcing the Defender to sample the wrong logs or misattribute the cause of an observed state change.
- [L1443] *   **Metrics:** Does the Defender look in the wrong place? Does discovering the anomaly cause a local correction or global model failure?

#### Source unit 1494 — dialogue lines 5986–5989
**Speaker/provenance:** assistant/model response. **Status:** critique/caveat/audit; claims remain scoped to their evidence.
- [L5986] - 8 hypotheses `H0…H7`, uniform prior.
- [L5987] - 6 tests. Each test is a partition of the 8 hypotheses into outcome classes.
- [L5988] - Facts are **exclusions**: `"the mechanism is not one of {Hi, Hj}"`. The presenter reorders/emphasises these.
- [L5989] - **The optimal test depends on which hypotheses the already-seen facts leave standing.** There is no test-independent "most outcomes" heuristic. This is the whole point: the best next question is a function of the current information state $h_t$, so a presenter that reshapes which facts land first can shift the optimum.
#### Source unit 1495 — dialogue lines 5991–5991

- [L5991] **Done-when:** over 200 semantic seeds, the identity of the argmax test varies across seeds *and* across the fact-prefixes a presentation induces; a "most-outcomes" heuristic scores strictly worse than the oracle on ≥40% of seeds.
